This holds for all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}\left(\left(\mathbb{T}\times\left(\frac{m}{2L},\frac{m+1}{2L}\right)\right)\times I\right)
, and so as a distribution
f(y_{t}(x),t)\in L^{1}L^{1}
is independent of
t\in I
, see for example Theorem 3.1.4’ in
[
31
]
. Therefore, there must exists some
g_{m}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}\times\left(\frac{m}{2L},\frac{m+1}{2L}\right))
such that (for a.e.
t\in I
)

f(y_{t}(x),t)=g_{m}(x)\text{ a.e. on }\mathbb{T}\times\left(\frac{m}{2L},\frac{m+1}{2L}\right).

Repeating for each
m\in\{0,1,...2L-1\}
gives some
g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
such that,

f(y_{t}(x),t)=g(x)\text{ a.e. on }\mathbb{T}^{2}.

And so
f(\cdot,t)=g\circ y_{t}^{-1}
is a Lagrangian solution to (
TE
) (Definition
2.6
), and hence also a renormalised weak solution (Definition
2.4
, Remark
2.7
).

To prove that
g\circ y_{t}^{-1}\in C^{0}((-\infty,\infty);L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
we shall use the fact that
y_{t}
is bijective, measure preserving, and
1
-Lipschitz in time, i.e. for all
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
,
t,s\in\mathbb{R}

\left|y_{t}(x)-y_{s}(x)\right|\leq|t-s|,

and so, by replacing
x
with
y_{s}^{-1}(x)
,

(23)

\left|y_{t}\circ y_{s}^{-1}(x)-x\right|\leq|t-s|.

The following follows a standard argument. We fix
t\in I
and write

g\circ y_{s}^{-1}=\left(g\circ y_{t}^{-1}\right)\circ y_{t}\circ y_{s}^{-1}.

Now mollify
g\circ y_{t}^{-1}
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, that is for each
\epsilon>0
take some
\phi_{\epsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
such that

\left\|\phi_{\epsilon}-g\circ y_{t}^{-1}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\leq\epsilon.

But also, since
y_{t}
,
y_{s}^{-1}
are measure preserving,

\left\|\phi_{\epsilon}\circ y_{t}\circ y_{s}^{-1}-g\circ y_{s}^{-1}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\leq\epsilon.

Then since
\phi_{\epsilon}
is uniformly continuous on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
, by (
23
) there exists
\delta\in(0,1)
such that if
|t-s|<\delta
then
\|\phi_{\epsilon}\circ y_{t}\circ y_{s}^{-1}-\phi_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{1}}\leq\epsilon
, and so

\|g\circ y_{t}^{-1}-g\circ y_{s}^{-1}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\leq 3\epsilon,

which completes the proof.
∎

Next, we demonstrate how these shear flows self-cancel. It is this property that gives rise to non-uniqueness in our later construction. This behaviour is quite different to recent works on anomalous dissipation,
[
24
,
26
]
, and even
[
15
,
5
]
, which also achieve some non-uniqueness of the vanishing viscosity limit. Contrary to our approach, these constructions aim to exploit some mixing effect of shear flows, and non-uniqueness occurs by exploiting different mixing along different viscosity subsequences. In contrast, the commutator relation below ensures that shear flows in our construction precisely reverse mixing that occurs previously. Non-uniqueness instead occurs by quantitatively different transport along different viscosity subsequences rather than by quantitatively different mixing along different viscosity subsequences.

Proposition 5.4

(Cancellation)
.

Let
L_{1},L_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
, and
i_{1},i_{2}\in\{1,2\}
such that
i_{1}\neq i_{2}
. Suppose that
2\tau_{2}=\frac{1}{2L_{1}}
, and that
2L_{2}\tau_{1}
is an odd integer.

Then the composition

\displaystyle\left(y_{\tau_{2}}^{(i_{2};L_{2})}\right)^{2}\circ y_{\tau_{1}}^{(i_{1};L_{1})}\circ\left(y_{\tau_{2}}^{(i_{2};L_{2})}\right)^{2}\circ y_{\tau_{1}}^{(i_{1};L_{1})}=\mathrm{Id},

where we have denoted by
f^{2}:=f\circ f
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, suppose
i_{1}=1,i_{2}=2
.

Since
\left(y_{\tau_{2}}^{(2;L_{2})}\right)^{2}=y_{2\tau_{2}}^{(2;L_{2})}
we instead prove the more general result

\displaystyle y_{\tau_{2}}^{(2;L_{2})}\circ y_{\tau_{1}}^{(1;L_{1})}\circ y_{\tau_{2}}^{(2;L_{2})}\circ y_{\tau_{1}}^{(1;L_{1})}=\mathrm{Id},

whenever both
2L_{1}\tau_{2}
and
2L_{2}\tau_{1}
are odd integers.

To this end, we divide
\mathbb{T}^{2}
into tiles as follows. Given
x\in[0,1)^{2}
we find the unique integers
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

(24)

x\in\left[\frac{m_{1}}{2L_{2}},\frac{m_{1}+1}{2L_{2}}\right)\times\left[\frac{m_{2}}{2L_{1}},\frac{m_{2}+1}{2L_{1}}\right).

We are concerned with the parity of
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
(even or odd), that is we have four ‘colours’ on our tiling, two for each coordinate.

This defines two equivalence relations
\left[\cdot\right]_{m_{1}},\left[\cdot\right]_{m_{2}}
on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
, each with two equivalence classes corresponding to the parity of either
m_{1}
or
m_{2}
in (
24
).

Since
2L_{1}\tau_{2}
,
2L_{2}\tau_{1}
are odd integers, the action of
y_{\tau_{1}}^{(1;L_{1})}
changes the parity of
m_{1}
and not
m_{2}
, while the action of
y_{\tau_{2}}^{(2;L_{2})}
changes the parity of
m_{2}
and not
m_{1}
.

Introduce the shorthand

\displaystyle x^{(0)}=x,

\displaystyle x^{(1)}=y_{\tau_{1}}^{(1;L_{1})}(x^{(0)}),

\displaystyle x^{(2)}=y_{\tau_{2}}^{(2;L_{2})}(x^{(1)}),

\displaystyle x^{(3)}=y_{\tau_{1}}^{(1;L_{1})}(x^{(2)}),

\displaystyle x^{(4)}=y_{\tau_{2}}^{(2;L_{2})}(x^{(3)}).

Then, by the above discussion,

\displaystyle\left[x^{(2)}\right]_{m_{2}}\neq\left[x^{(0)}\right]_{m_{2}},

\displaystyle\left[x^{(3)}\right]_{m_{1}}\neq\left[x^{(1)}\right]_{m_{1}}.

From the definitions of
y_{\tau_{1}}^{(1;L_{1})},y_{\tau_{2}}^{(2;L_{2})}
this implies

\displaystyle x^{(3)}-x^{(2)}=-\left(x^{(1)}-x^{(0)}\right),

\displaystyle x^{(4)}-x^{(3)}=-\left(x^{(2)}-x^{(1)}\right),

and hence
x^{(4)}=x^{(0)}
as required.
∎

Now for some sequences
\{L_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\{i_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,
\{\tau_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
to be specified later, we first choose the order in which to apply these shear flows
y_{\tau_{k}}^{(i_{k};L_{k})}
. We use a double index
(k,m)\in\mathcal{D}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, where
k
shall index the parameters of the Lagrangian shear
y_{\tau_{k}}^{(i_{k};L_{k})}
, while
m
denotes the
m
th
occurrence of that particular shear.

To exploit Proposition
5.4
, we shall ensure that there are exactly two occurrences of a suitable shear
y_{\tau_{k+1}}^{(i_{k+1};L_{k+1})}
between each occurrence of
y_{\tau_{k}}^{(i_{k};L_{k})}
. If we denote by
t_{k,m}\in[0,1]
the times at which the shears are first applied, we may illustrate this construction in the following diagram:

t=0
t=1
{t_{1,0}}
{t_{1,1}}
{t_{2,0}}
{t_{2,1}}
{t_{2,2}}
{t_{2,3}}
{t_{3,0}}\;t_{3,1}
...
...
{t_{3,2}}\;t_{3,3}
...
...
{t_{3,4}}\;t_{3,5}
...
...
{t_{3,6}}\;t_{3,7}
...
...
f_{0}
\circ y_{-\tau_{1}}^{(i_{1};L_{1})}
\circ y_{-\tau_{1}}^{(i_{1};L_{1})}
\circ y_{-\tau_{2}}^{(i_{2};L_{2})}
...
\circ y_{-\tau_{2}}^{(i_{2};L_{2})}
...
\circ y_{-\tau_{2}}^{(i_{2};L_{2})}
...
\circ y_{-\tau_{2}}^{(i_{2};L_{2})}
...
=f(\cdot,1)

If we include only the shears
y_{\tau_{k}}^{(i_{k};L_{k})}
for
k\leq K
, it can now be seen how Proposition
5.4
may create two different behaviours of the trace
f(\cdot,1)
as
2K\to\infty
, and
2K+1\to\infty
.

The frequencies of the shears,
L_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
, will later be chosen to grow sufficiently quickly so that advection-diffusion (

ν

-ADE
) along a viscosity subsequence
\nu_{k}>0
will only include the effect of the first
k
shears, as per Theorem
4.3
.

To this end, we first define a total order
<_{\mathrm{time}}
on the indexing set
\mathcal{D}
, and then define
t_{k,m}
so that they respect this ordering.

Definition 5.5

(Lexicographic dyadic ordering)
.

We define the following set of ‘dyadic’ pairs

\mathcal{D}=\{(k,m):k\in\mathbb{N},m\in\mathbb{Z},0\leq m<2^{k}\},

and define a total order
<_{\mathrm{time}}
on
\mathcal{D}
via

(25)

(k_{1},m_{1})<_{\mathrm{time}}(k_{2},m_{2})\text{ if and only if }\begin{cases}m_{1}2^{-k_{1}}<m_{2}2^{-k_{2}},\text{ or}\\
m_{1}2^{-k_{1}}=m_{2}2^{-k_{2}}\text{ and }k_{1}<k_{2}.\end{cases}

Define also for each
K\in\mathbb{N}
the finite subset

\mathcal{D}_{K}=\{(k,m)\in\mathcal{D}:k\leq K\},

which inherits the total order
(\mathcal{D},<_{\mathrm{time}})
, which is now a finite order.

We define for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
with
0\leq m<2^{k}
,

(26)

t_{k,m}=\sum_{(k^{\prime},m^{\prime})<_{\mathrm{time}}(k,m)}2^{-2k^{\prime}}<1,

where the bound on
t_{k,m}
follows from direct calculation of
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}2^{k}2^{-2k}=1
, and an empty sum is zero.

We illustrate this arrangement in the following diagram:

(27)

t=0
t=1
{t_{1,0}}
{t_{1,1}}
{t_{2,0}}
{t_{2,1}}
{t_{2,2}}
{t_{2,3}}
{t_{3,0}}\;t_{3,1}
...
...
{t_{3,2}}\;t_{3,3}
...
...
{t_{3,4}}\;t_{3,5}
...
...
{t_{3,6}}\;t_{3,7}
...
...
2^{-2}
2^{-2}
2^{-4}
...
2^{-4}
...
2^{-4}
...
2^{-4}
...

Next, we construct the ‘fractal’ vector fields exploiting the above.

Definition 5.6

(Fractal shear flow)
.

Consider a sequence of tuples
\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
with
i_{k}\in\{1,2\}
,
L_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\tau_{k}>0
.

(i)

We say
\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies the Finiteness Condition if for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have

(28)

\text{(Finiteness Condition)\quad}\tau_{k}<2^{-2k}.

(ii)

We say
\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies the Cancellation Conditions if for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have

(29)

\text{(Cancellation Conditions)\quad}\begin{gathered}i_{k+1}\neq i_{k},\\
2\tau_{k+1}=\frac{1}{2L_{k}},\\
2L_{k+1}\tau_{k}\text{ an odd integer}.\end{gathered}

Following the notation of Definition
5.5
, in particular (
26
), when
\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfy the Finiteness Condition (
28
), we may define for each
K\in\mathbb{N}
the following fractal shear flows on
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\displaystyle u_{K}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k=1}^{K}}(x,t)=\begin{cases}u^{(i_{k};L_{k})}(x)&\text{if $t\in[t_{k,m},t_{k,m}+\tau_{k}]$ for some $(k,m)\in\mathcal{D}_{K}$},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

\displaystyle u_{\infty}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}}(x,t)=\begin{cases}u^{(i_{k};L_{k})}(x)&\text{if $t\in[t_{k,m},t_{k,m}+\tau_{k}]$ for some $(k,m)\in\mathcal{D}$},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

By (
26
), this is well-defined since the Finiteness Condition (
28
) implies that the intervals
[t_{k,m},t_{k,m}+\tau_{k}]
are disjoint subsets of
[0,1]
.

Next, we show how this construction exploits Proposition
5.4
to create non-uniqueness of renormalised solutions to (
TE
).

Proposition 5.7

(Fractal behaviour)
.

We follow the notation introduced in Definitions
5.5
,
5.6
.

Let
f_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, and suppose we have an infinite sequence of tuples
\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
with
i_{k}\in\{1,2\}
,
L_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\tau_{k}>0
, satisfying the Finiteness Condition (
28
). Then for each
K\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a unique weak solution
f^{K}
to (
TE
) along
u_{K}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k=1}^{K}}
with initial data
f_{0}
. Moreover,
f^{K}\in(C^{0}L^{1})\cap(L^{\infty}L^{\infty})
, is a Lagrangian solution, and hence also a renormalised weak solution (Definitions
2.4
,
2.6
).

If in addition
\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfy the Cancellation Conditions (
29
) then

\displaystyle f^{2K}\xrightarrow{K\to\infty}f^{\mathrm{even}},

\displaystyle f^{2K+1}\xrightarrow{K\to\infty}f^{\mathrm{odd}},

with the above convergence in weak-
*

L^{\infty}L^{\infty}
, and strong in
L^{p}L^{\infty}
for any
p\in[1,\infty)
. Moreover, the sequences
f^{2K}\in C^{0}L^{1}
and
f^{2K+1}\in C^{0}L^{1}
are eventually constant as
K\to\infty
when restricted to the spatio-temporal domain
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[t_{k,m},t_{k,m}+2^{-2k}]
for any
(k,m)\in\mathcal{D}
, or to the spatio-temporal domain
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\{1\}
. Furthermore, the limit functions
f^{\mathrm{even}},f^{\mathrm{odd}}
are renormalised weak solutions to (
TE
) along
u_{\infty}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}}
with initial data
f_{0}
.

If
f_{0}
is not constant, then
f^{\mathrm{even}}\neq f^{\mathrm{odd}}
, and in particular,

(30)

\begin{gathered}f^{\mathrm{even}}(\cdot,1)=f_{0},\\
f^{\mathrm{odd}}(\cdot,1)=f_{0}\circ y_{-2\tau_{1}}^{(i_{1};L_{1})}.\end{gathered}

Proof.

Recall the language and notation introduced in Definitions
5.5
,
5.6
, as well as Definitions
2.4
,
2.6
of renormalised and Lagrangian solutions to (
TE
).

By Theorems
3.1
,
3.4
, there exists some weak solution
f^{K}\in C_{\mathrm{weak-}*}^{0}L^{\infty}
to (
TE
) along
u_{K}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k=1}^{K}}
with initial data
f_{0}
.

We aim to show
f\in C^{0}L^{1}
with

(31)

f^{K}(\cdot,t)=f_{0}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t}^{K}\big)^{-1},

for
\big\{\tilde{y}_{t}^{K}\big\}_{t\in[0,1]}
a Lagrangian flow along
u_{K}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k=1}^{K}}
which does not depend on
f_{0}
, thus proving uniqueness.

From the definition of
u_{K}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k=1}^{K}}
, finiteness of the set
\mathcal{D}_{K}
, and disjointness of the intervals
[t_{k,m},t_{k,m}+\tau_{k}]
for all
(k,m)\in\mathcal{D}_{K}
, we may piecewise apply Proposition
5.3
to
f^{K}
. We use that
f^{K}\in C_{\mathrm{weak-}*}^{0}L^{\infty}
to glue together the pieces, and that any weak solution to (
TE
) along
u\equiv 0
on an open time interval
I
is a constant function of
t\in I
, see for example Theorem 3.1.4’ in
[
31
]
. This constructs for each
K\in\mathbb{N}
a Lagrangian flow
\tilde{y}_{t}^{K}
satisfying (
31
), and moreover gives an expression for
\tilde{y}_{t}^{K}
in terms of the Lagrangian shear flows
y_{t}^{(i_{k};L_{k})}
, see (
33
) below.

For any
(k,m)\in\mathcal{D}_{K}
, define

t_{\mathrm{suc}_{K}(k,m)}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $(k,m)$ is maximal in $(\mathcal{D}_{K},<_{\mathrm{time}})$, else}\\
t_{\mathcal{S}}&\text{for $\mathcal{S}\in\mathcal{D}_{K}$ the successor of $(k,m)$ in $(\mathcal{D}_{K},<_{\mathrm{time}})$}.\end{cases}

Note from (
26
), and the Finiteness Condition (
28
), that

(32)

\tau_{k}<2^{-2k}\leq t_{\mathrm{suc}_{K}(k,m)}-t_{k,m}.

Therefore, for each
t\in[t_{k,m},t_{\mathrm{suc}_{K}(k,m)}]
,

(33)

\tilde{y}_{t}^{K}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t_{k,m}}^{K}\big)^{-1}=\begin{cases}y_{t-t_{k,m}}^{(i_{k};L_{k})}&\text{if }t\in[t_{k,m},t_{k,m}+\tau_{k}],\\
y_{\tau_{k}}^{(i_{k};L_{k})}&\text{if }t\in[t_{k,m}+\tau_{k},t_{\mathrm{suc}_{K}(k,m)}].\end{cases}

This expression defines
\tilde{y}_{t}^{K}
for all
t\in[0,1]
, since
t_{1,0}=0
and
\tilde{y}_{0}^{K}=\mathrm{Id}
. This completes the proof of uniqueness.

Next, by considering (
25
), (
26
), we may show that (see the illustration (
27
))

(34)

t_{\mathrm{suc}_{K}(k,m)}=\begin{cases}t_{k,m}+2^{-2k}&\text{if }k<K,\\
t_{k,m}+2^{1-2k}&\text{if }k=K.\\
\end{cases}

Moreover, for all
(k,m)\in\mathcal{D}
,

(35)

\displaystyle t_{k,m}+2^{-2k}

\displaystyle=t_{k+1,2m},

\displaystyle t_{k,m}+2^{1-2k}

\displaystyle=t_{k,m+1}\quad\text{when $m$ even}.

Assume that additionally
\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfy the Cancellation Conditions (
29
).

Fix some
K\in\mathbb{N}
. We claim that, for each
(k,m)\in\mathcal{D}_{K}
, and for all
\left.t\in[t_{k,m},t_{k,m}+2^{-2k}]\right.
, one has as maps
\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
,

(36)

\tilde{y}_{t}^{K+2}=\tilde{y}_{t}^{K}.

By (
32
), (
33
), it is sufficient to show, for all
(k,m)\in\mathcal{D}_{K}
, that as maps
\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
,

\tilde{y}_{t_{\mathrm{suc}_{K}(k,m)}}^{K+2}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t_{\mathrm{suc}_{K+2}(k,m)}}^{K+2}\big)^{-1}=\tilde{y}_{t_{\mathrm{suc}_{K}(k,m)}}^{K}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t_{\mathrm{suc}_{K+2}(k,m)}}^{K}\big)^{-1}.

By (
34
), for all
k<K
this is immediate. Meanwhile, by (
34
), (
35
), for
k=K
, we may rewrite this, and now need to prove that for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
with
0\leq m<2^{K}
,

\tilde{y}_{t_{K+1,2m}+2^{-2K}}^{K+2}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t_{K+1,2m}}^{K+2}\big)^{-1}=\tilde{y}_{t_{K+1,2m}+2^{-2K}}^{K}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t_{K+1,2m}}^{K}\big)^{-1}.

In particular, it is certainly sufficient to show, for any
(k,m)\in\mathcal{D}_{K+1}
with
m
even, that as maps
\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
,

(37)

\tilde{y}_{t_{k,m}+2^{2-2k}}^{K}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t_{k,m}}^{K}\big)^{-1}\begin{aligned} &=\begin{cases}y_{2\tau_{k}}^{(i_{k};L_{k})}&\text{if }k=K\;(\mathrm{mod}\;2),\\
\mathrm{Id}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}\\
&=:Y_{k,m}^{K},\end{aligned}

where we have defined the shorthand
Y_{k,m}^{K}:\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
.

Fixing an even
m\in\mathbb{Z}
with
0\leq m<2^{K+1}
, we prove this by induction. For
k=K+1
, by (
32
), (
33
) we see that as maps
\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{T}^{2}
,

\tilde{y}_{t_{\mathrm{suc}_{K}(k,m)}}^{K}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t_{\mathrm{suc}_{K+2}(k,m)}}^{K}\big)^{-1}=\mathrm{Id}.

After rewriting this using (
34
), (
35
), this proves (
37
) for
k=K+1
.

Now let
k\leq K
. By (
35
), and since by assumption
m
is even, we may rewrite (it may be helpful to consider the illustration (
27
))

Y_{k,m}^{K}=\begin{aligned} &\left(\tilde{y}_{t_{k+1,2m+2}+2^{-2k}}^{K}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t_{k+1,2m+2}}^{K}\big)^{-1}\right)\\
&\circ\left(\tilde{y}_{t_{k,m+1}+2^{-2k}}^{K}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t_{k,m+1}}^{K}\big)^{-1}\right)\\
&\circ\left(\tilde{y}_{t_{k+1,2m}+2^{-2k}}^{K}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t_{k+1,2m}}^{K}\big)^{-1}\right)\\
&\circ\left(\tilde{y}_{t_{k,m}+2^{-2k}}^{K}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t_{k,m}}^{K}\big)^{-1}\right).\end{aligned}

Then by the definition of
Y_{k+1,m^{\prime}}^{K}
, (
32
), and (
33
), the right hand side may be rewritten again as

Y_{k,m}^{K}=Y_{k+1,2m+2}^{K}\circ y_{\tau_{k}}^{(i_{k};L_{k})}\circ Y_{k+1,2m}^{K}\circ y_{\tau_{k}}^{(i_{k};L_{k})}.

By Proposition
5.4
, the result (
37
) for
k
now follows from the same result for
k+1
. This completes the induction, and thus the proof of (
37
), and so also the proof of (
36
).

Next, note by (
26
), that

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=\left(\bigcup_{(k,m)\in\mathcal{D}}[t_{k,m},t_{k,m}+2^{-2k}]\right)\cup\left\{t_{k,m}+2^{2-2k}:(k,m)\in\mathcal{D}\text{ with $m$ even}\right\}

\displaystyle\subset[0,1],

has Lebesgue-measure 1. Moreover, by (
36
), (
37
), the sequences
f^{2K}
,
f^{2K+1}
are eventually constant on
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[t_{k,m},t_{k,m}+2^{-2k}]
, and each
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\{t_{k,m}+2^{2-2k}\}
with
m
even.

Therefore, for all
t\in E
, and so for a.e.
t\in[0,1]
, we see that

(38)

\begin{gathered}f^{2K}(\cdot,t)\xrightarrow{K\to\infty}f_{0}\circ\big(\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{even}}_{t}\big)^{-1},\\
f^{2K+1}(\cdot,t)\xrightarrow{K\to\infty}f_{0}\circ\big(\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{odd}}_{t}\big)^{-1},\end{gathered}

for some Lebesgue-measure preserving
\{\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{even}}_{t}\}_{t\in E}
,
\{\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{odd}}_{t}\}_{t\in E}
independent of
f_{0}
, with the convergence strong in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
.

Moreover, by the dominated convergence Theorem, we see that (
38
) converges strongly in
L^{p}(E;L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
for all
p\in[1,\infty)
, and hence also in weak-
*

L^{\infty}(E;L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
.

By Definition
5.6
, for all
t\in[0,1]
(in particular
t\in E
),
u_{K}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k=1}^{K}}(\cdot,t)
is bounded by 1 in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and as
K\to\infty
the sequence is eventually equal to
u_{\infty}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}}(\cdot,t)
. Therefore it too converges strongly in
L^{p}(E;L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}))
for all
p\in[1,\infty)
.

Fix
t\in E
, and apply the Trace Formula (
1
) to
f^{2K}
,
f^{2K+1}
. Taking now the limit
K\to\infty
then gives that for all
t\in E
, for any
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times[0,T])
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}f_{0}\circ\big(\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{even}}_{t}\big)^{-1}\phi(\cdot,t)\;dx\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}f_{0}\phi_{0}\;dx+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\left(E\cap[0,t]\right)}f_{0}\circ\big(\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{even}}_{t}\big)^{-1}\left(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}+u_{\infty}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}}\cdot\nabla\phi+\nu\Delta\phi\right)dxdt,\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}f_{0}\circ\big(\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{odd}}_{t}\big)^{-1}\phi(\cdot,t)\;dx\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}f_{0}\phi_{0}\;dx+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\left(E\cap[0,t]\right)}f_{0}\circ\big(\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{odd}}_{t}\big)^{-1}\left(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}+u_{\infty}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}}\cdot\nabla\phi+\nu\Delta\phi\right)dxdt,\end{aligned}

where
\phi_{0}=\phi(\cdot,0)
.

This implies that
f_{0}\circ\big(\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{even}}_{t}\big)^{-1},f_{0}\circ\big(\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{odd}}_{t}\big)^{-1}\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{weak-}*}(E;L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
and so may be extended to
f^{\mathrm{even}},f^{\mathrm{odd}}\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{weak-}*}([0,1];L^{\infty})
, as argued in Theorem
3.1
.

For any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,1))
let
t=1
in the above (noting that
1=t_{1,0}+2^{2-2}\in E
). This proves that
f^{\mathrm{even}}
,
f^{\mathrm{odd}}
are both weak solutions to (
TE
) along
u_{\infty}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}}
with initial data
f_{0}
.

Moreover, for any
\beta\in C_{b}^{0}(\mathbb{R})
, we may rewrite
\beta\big(f_{0}\circ\big(\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{even}}_{t}\big)^{-1}\big)=\beta(f_{0})\circ\big(\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{even}}_{t}\big)^{-1}
. By repeating the above arguments we have also that
\beta(f_{0})\circ\big(\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{even}}_{t}\big)^{-1}
(can be extended to) a weak solution to (
TE
) along
u_{\infty}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}}
with initial data
\beta(f_{0})
. Therefore, we see that
f^{\mathrm{even}}
is a renormalised weak solution to (
TE
) along
u_{\infty}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}}
with initial data
f_{0}
. Similarly for
f^{\mathrm{odd}}
.

Next, observe that by (
37
), (
38
), for all
(k,m)\in\mathcal{D}
with
m
even, that

(39)

\begin{gathered}f^{\mathrm{even}}(\cdot,t_{k,m}+2^{2-2k})=f^{\mathrm{even}}(\cdot,t_{k,m})\circ\begin{cases}\big(y_{2\tau_{k}}^{(i_{k};L_{k})}\big)^{-1}&\text{for even }k,\\
\mathrm{Id}&\text{for odd }k.\end{cases}\\
f^{\mathrm{odd}}(\cdot,t_{k,m}+2^{2-2k})=f^{\mathrm{odd}}(\cdot,t_{k,m})\circ\begin{cases}\mathrm{Id}&\text{for even }k,\\
\big(y_{2\tau_{k}}^{(i_{k};L_{k})}\big)^{-1}&\text{for odd }k.\end{cases}\end{gathered}

In particular, since
t_{1,0}=0
, we have proved (
30
).

It remains to show, when
f_{0}
is not constant, that from (
39
) we deduce
f^{\mathrm{even}}\neq f^{\mathrm{odd}}
. Assume to the contrary that they are equal and call this solution
f
. Then by (
39
) (noting that by (
25
), (
26
),
1-2^{2-2k}=t_{k,2^{k}-2}
, see for example the illustration (
27
)), we have for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, that

\displaystyle f(\cdot,1)=f(\cdot,1-2^{2-2k}),

\displaystyle f(\cdot,1)=f(\cdot,1-2^{2-2k})\circ\big(y_{2\tau_{k}}^{(i_{k};L_{k})}\big)^{-1}.

In particular, taking
k=1
, we see
f(\cdot,1)=f_{0}
. Substituting this back in gives
f_{0}=f(\cdot,1-2^{2-2k})
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Again, substituting this back in gives that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

f_{0}=f_{0}\circ y_{2\tau_{k}}^{(i_{k};L_{k})}.

In terms of the unit vectors
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
, we have that for a.e.
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
,
f_{0}(x)=f_{0}(x+2\tau_{k}e_{i_{k}})
. Convolving with a smooth function
\rho\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
then gives that for all
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
,

(40)

(\rho*f_{0})(x)=(\rho*f_{0})(x+2\tau_{k}e_{i_{k}}).

By the Finiteness and Cancellation Conditions (
28
), (
29
), we see that
\tau_{k}\xrightarrow{k\to\infty}0
, and
i_{k+1}\neq i_{k}
(so equal to both
1
and
2
infinitely often). Now
(\rho*f_{0})\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, and so by (
40
) we must have that
\rho*f_{0}
is a constant. This holds for all
\rho\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and therefore implies also that
f_{0}
is a constant, reaching the required contradiction.
∎

Finally, for a suitably fast growing sequence
\{L_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{N}
, we apply Theorem
4.3
to the sequence of vector fields
u_{n}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k=1}^{n}}(x,t)
. This allows us to control the vanishing viscosity limit along the vector field
u_{\infty}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}}
.

Theorem 5.8

(Non-unique renormalised vanishing viscosity limit solutions)
.

There exists a divergence-free vector field
u\in L^{\infty}([0,1];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}))
, and a sequence
\{\nu_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\nu_{n}>0
and
\nu_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
, such that for any initial data
f_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, and for
f^{\nu}
the unique solution to (

ν

-ADE
) along
u
with initial data
f_{0}
, one has

\displaystyle f^{\nu_{2n}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}f^{\mathrm{even}},

\displaystyle f^{\nu_{2n+1}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}f^{\mathrm{odd}},

with the above convergence in weak-
*

L^{\infty}([0,1];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
, and strong in
L^{p}([0,1];L^{p}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
for all
p\in[1,\infty)
. Furthermore, the limit functions
f^{\mathrm{even}},f^{\mathrm{odd}}
are renormalised weak solutions to (
TE
) along
u
with initial data
f_{0}
.

If
f_{0}
is not constant, then
f^{\mathrm{even}}\neq f^{\mathrm{odd}}
, and moreover the set of weak-
*
limit points of
f^{\nu}\in L^{\infty}([0,1];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
as
\nu\to 0
is uncountable.

Proof.

Recall the language and notation introduced in Definitions
5.5
,
5.6
, as well as Definitions
2.4
,
2.6
of renormalised and Lagrangian solutions to (
TE
).

Consider any infinite sequence of tuples
\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
with
i_{k}\in\{1,2\}
,
L_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\tau_{k}>0
, and satisfying the Finiteness Condition (
28
).

By Proposition
5.7
there exists a Lagrangian solution
f^{n}\in C^{0}L^{1}
, unique in the class of all weak solutions, to (
TE
) along
u_{n}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k=1}^{n}}
for any initial data
f_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
. Moreover,
u_{n}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k=1}^{n}}
is bounded by
1
in
L^{\infty}([0,1];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}))
.

Let
d_{*}
be a metric inducing the weak-
*
topology on

X=\left\{u\in L^{\infty}\left([0,1];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})\right):\left\|u\right\|_{L^{\infty}L^{\infty}}\leq 1\right\}.

Let
f_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, and denote for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\nu>0
, by
f^{n,\nu}
, respectively
f^{n}
, the unique weak solution to (

ν

-ADE
), respectively (
TE
), along
u_{n}
with initial data
f_{0}
. Moreover denote by
f^{\infty,\nu}
the unique weak solution to (

ν

-ADE
) along
u_{\infty}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}}
with initial data
f_{0}
.

Then by Theorem
4.3
,

S.1

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
\nu_{n}>0
,
\epsilon_{n}>0
depending only on
\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k=1}^{n}
(and in particular not on
f_{0}
), with
\nu_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0
monotonically, such that the following holds true:

S.5

If
d_{*}(u_{n+1},u_{n})\leq\epsilon_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then for all
p\in[1,\infty)

\left\|f^{\infty,\nu_{n}}-f^{n}\right\|_{L^{\infty}L^{p}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.

We now construct such a sequence
\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. Let
(i_{1},L_{1},\tau_{1})=(1,2^{2},2^{-2})
. We proceed by induction on
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume
\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k=1}^{n}
are given, and satisfy for
k\in\{1,...,n\}
.

\begin{gathered}i_{k}\in\{1,2\},\\
L_{k}\in\mathbb{N},\\
L_{k}\geq 2^{2k},\\
0<\tau_{k}\leq 2^{-2k},\end{gathered}

It is straightforward to check that this is satisfied for the base case
(i_{1},L_{1},\tau_{1})=(1,2^{2},2^{-2})
.

If
n\geq 2
, we assume in the inductive hypothesis that also, for
k\in\{1,...,n-1\}
,

\begin{gathered}i_{k+1}\neq i_{k},\\
2\tau_{k+1}=\frac{1}{2L_{k}},\\
2L_{k+1}\tau_{k}\;\text{is an odd integer},\\
d_{*}\left(u_{k+1}^{\{(i_{l},L_{l},\tau_{l})\}_{l=1}^{k+1}},u_{k}^{\{(i_{l},L_{l},\tau_{l})\}_{l=1}^{k}}\right)\leq\epsilon_{k}.\end{gathered}

We then choose
(i_{n+1},L_{n+1},\tau_{n+1})
as follows. Let
i_{n+1}\in\{1,2\}\setminus\{i_{n}\}
. Let
\tau_{n+1}=\frac{1}{4L_{n}}
which by the inductive hypothesis satisfies
\tau_{n+1}\leq 2^{-2k-2}
. Let

(41)

L_{n+1}=2L_{n-1}(2M+1),

for some large
M\in\mathbb{N}
to be chosen, where when
n=1
we take
L_{0}=1
. This will ensure
2L_{n+1}\tau_{n}
is an odd integer.

By (
22
) and Definition
5.6
, we see for
n\in\mathbb{N}
fixed, that

d_{*}\left(u_{n+1}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k=1}^{n+1}},u_{n}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k=1}^{n}}\right)\xrightarrow{(L_{n+1})\to\infty}0.

Therefore, by taking
M\in\mathbb{N}
large enough in (
41
), we have that both
L_{n+1}\geq 2^{2n+2}
, and

d_{*}\left(u_{n+1}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k=1}^{n+1}},u_{n}^{\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k=1}^{n}}\right)\leq\epsilon_{n},

completing the induction.

We have constructed
\{(i_{k},L_{k},\tau_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfying the Finiteness and Cancellation Conditions (
28
), (
29
), and also (
S.1
), (
S.5
). The main statement of the theorem is now a straightforward corollary of Proposition
5.7
.

Finally we show that, if
f_{0}
is not constant, then the set of weak-
*
limit points of
f^{\nu}\in L^{\infty}([0,1];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
as
\nu\to 0
is uncountable. Working in the weak-
*
topology of
L^{\infty}L^{\infty}
, the set of vanishing viscosity limit points is bounded, and so is a metric space. Moreover, the map
\nu\to f^{\nu}
is continuous, which implies that the set of limit points is connected. If it is a connected metric space, then it is either a singleton or uncountable. Therefore, we conclude by observing that
f^{\mathrm{even}}\neq f^{\mathrm{odd}}
are at least two limit points.
∎

6.
Inadmissibility

6.1.
Notation

We continue with the same notation for the 2-torus introduced in Section
5.1
. However, we no longer make use of the notation in Definitions
5.2
,
5.5
,
5.6
. In addition, we define the binary expansion of some
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
as follows. For the representative
(x_{1},x_{2})\in[0,1)^{2}
denote for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, by
x_{i,k}
the
k
th
binary digit of the
i
th
coordinate of
x
. That is, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
,

(42)

x_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}x_{i,k}2^{-k},

where
x_{i,k}\in\{0,1\}
and
x_{i,k}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 14.29163pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{k\to\infty}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 14.29163pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{k\to\infty}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 10.448pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{k\to\infty}}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 10.448pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\xrightarrow{k\to\infty}}}}1
(as is standard to ensure uniqueness of the binary expansion).

First, we define vector fields and corresponding Lagrangian flows, which swap points in
\mathbb{T}^{2}
according to their binary expansion. These ‘binary swaps’ form the building block of our construction. Subsequently we shall give a divergence-free vector field in
L^{\infty}([0,100];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2}))
with
L^{\infty}L^{\infty}
-norm equal to 1, which perfectly mixes the transported scalar to its spatial average, and subsequently unmixes, any initial data to (
TE
). Our aim is then to show that this behaviour is the unique limit point of vanishing viscosity of the associated solution to (

ν

-ADE
). In order to ensure uniqueness of the limit points it is necessary to ‘gradually’ perform these binary swaps, that is they must be restricted to gradually smaller regions of space, see (
45
) below.

Definition 6.2

(Lagrangian binary swap)
.

Suppose
i\in\{1,2\}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\in\{1,...,2^{\left\lfloor k/2\right\rfloor}\}
,
L\in\mathbb{N}
, with
L\geq k+1
.

The proof below will define a time-dependent, divergence-free vector field called the
(i,k,n;L)
-binary swap

u^{(i,k,n;L)}:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,3\cdot 2^{-k}]\to\mathbb{R}^{2},

and a corresponding Lagrangian flow map (Definition
2.6
)
\big\{y^{(i,k,n;L)}_{t}\big\}_{t\in[0,3\cdot 2^{-k}]}
with the properties (
43
)-(
47
) below.

Define
J_{k,n}=\left[(n-1)2^{-\left\lfloor k/2\right\rfloor},n2^{-\left\lfloor k/2\right\rfloor}\right]\subset\mathbb{T}
. Then at time
t=3\cdot 2^{-k}
, for a.e.
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
,
y^{(i,k,n;L)}_{3\cdot 2^{-k}}
will swap the
k
th
and
(k+1)
th
binary digits of
x_{i}
if
x_{i}\in J_{k,n}
.

That is
y_{0}^{(i,k,n;L)}=\mathrm{Id}
. For a.e.
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
denote by
(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2})=y^{(i,k,n;L)}_{3\cdot 2^{-k}}(x)
. Following the notation for binary expansions in (
42
), for
j\in\{1,2\}
the coordinate, and for
l\in\mathbb{N}
the binary digit,

(43)

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{j}

\displaystyle=x_{j}\;\text{if}\;j\neq i,

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{i,l}

\displaystyle=x_{i,l}\;\text{for}\;l\notin\{k,k+1\},

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{i,k+1}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}x_{i,k}&\text{if }x_{i}\in J_{k,n},\\
x_{i,k+1}&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{i,k}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}x_{i,k+1}&\text{if }x_{i}\in J_{k,n},\\
x_{i,k}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Additionally, the vector field
u^{(i,k,n;L)}
will satisfy

(44)

\left\|u^{(i,k,n;L)}\right\|_{L^{\infty}L^{\infty}}\leq 1,

and for all
t\in[0,3\cdot 2^{-k}]
, and all
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
with
x_{i}\notin J_{k,n}
,

(45)

u^{(i,k,n;L)}(x,t)=0.

Moreover, for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
fixed, as
L\to\infty
,

(46)

u^{(i,k,n;L)}\xrightharpoonup{L\to\infty}0,\;\text{in weak-$*$ $L^{\infty}L^{\infty}$}.

Finally, for any
r,r^{\prime}\in\{1,...,2^{k-1}\}
, for the spatial intervals
J=[(r-1)2^{1-k},r2^{1-k}]
, and
J^{\prime}=[(r^{\prime}-1)2^{1-k},r^{\prime}2^{1-k}]
, the Lagrangian-flow
y^{(i,k,n;L)}_{t}
preserves the squares

(47)

y^{(i,k,n;L)}_{t}:J\times J^{\prime}\leftrightarrow J\times J^{\prime}.

Proof.

We now construct the above vector field and Lagrangian flow. We shall give the construction for
i=1
and then define its coordinate reflection for
i=2
. That is, for each
(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
,
t\in[0,3\cdot 2^{-k}]

(48)

\displaystyle u^{(2,k,n;L)}((x_{1},x_{2}),t)=u^{(1,k,n;L)}((x_{2},x_{1}),t),

(49)

\displaystyle y_{t}^{(2,k,n;L)}((x_{1},x_{2}))=y_{t}^{(1,k,n;L)}((x_{2},x_{1})).

so that (
44
), (
46
), (
43
) with
i=2
follow from the same result for
i=1
.

We shall achieve the required binary-swaps (
43
) by piecing together particular rotating vector fields which perform half rotations of rectangular regions of
\mathbb{T}^{2}
. Suppose
W,H>0
, and define the following 1-Lipschitz stream-function

\displaystyle\psi:\left(-\frac{W}{2},\frac{W}{2}\right)\times\left(-\frac{H}{2},\frac{H}{2}\right)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\psi((x_{1},x_{2}))=\min\{W,H\}\cdot\max\left\{\left(\frac{x_{1}}{W}\right)^{2},\left(\frac{x_{2}}{H}\right)^{2}\right\}.

This defines a 1-bounded, time-independent, divergence-free, vector field
\nabla^{\perp}\psi=\big(-\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial x_{2}},\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial x_{1}}\big)
on the open rectangle
\left(-\frac{W}{2},\frac{W}{2}\right)\times\left(-\frac{H}{2},\frac{H}{2}\right)
.
\nabla^{\perp}\psi
has rectangular flow lines, as illustrated in the following diagram:

(50)

W
H

Moreover, on each triangular segment,
\nabla^{\perp}\psi
is a linear function of space, and each flow line within each segment has a time period equal to
\max\{W,H\}
.

Therefore,
\nabla^{\perp}\psi
admits a Lagrangian flow
\big\{y_{t}\big\}_{t\in(-\infty,\infty)}
on
\left(-\frac{W}{2},\frac{W}{2}\right)\times\left(-\frac{H}{2},\frac{H}{2}\right)
. That is for all
t\in(-\infty,\infty)
,
y_{t}:\left(-\frac{W}{2},\frac{W}{2}\right)\times\left(-\frac{H}{2},\frac{H}{2}\right)\leftrightarrow\left(-\frac{W}{2},\frac{W}{2}\right)\times\left(-\frac{H}{2},\frac{H}{2}\right)
is a Lebesgue-measure preserving bijection,
y_{0}=\mathrm{Id}
, and for all
x\in\left(-\frac{W}{2},\frac{W}{2}\right)\times\left(-\frac{H}{2},\frac{H}{2}\right)
, and all
t\in(-\infty,\infty)
,
\frac{d}{dt}y_{t}(x)=\nabla^{\perp}\psi(y_{t}(x))
.

Moreover, we have
y_{0}=\mathrm{Id}
, while
y_{2\max\{W,H\}}
is exactly a half rotation of
\left(-\frac{W}{2},\frac{W}{2}\right)\times\left(-\frac{H}{2},\frac{H}{2}\right)
around its centre. Finally, both
y_{t}
and
y_{t}^{-1}
are Lipschitz in both
x
and
t
. That is, for all
x,x^{\prime}\in\left(-\frac{W}{2},\frac{W}{2}\right)\times\left(-\frac{H}{2},\frac{H}{2}\right)
, and all
t,s\in(-\infty,\infty)
,

(51)

\begin{gathered}\left|y_{t}(x)-y_{s}(x^{\prime})\right|\leq C(|x-x^{\prime}|+|t-s|),\\
\left|y_{t}^{-1}(x)-y_{s}^{-1}(x^{\prime})\right|\leq C(|x-x^{\prime}|+|t-s|),\end{gathered}

for some constant
C>0
.

Consider now any open rectangle
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with width
W
and height
H
. By translating (
50
) to
Q
, we have the same vector field on
Q
, which, to simplify the following presentation, we symbolically notate by

We iterate that this is a 1-bounded, time-independent, divergence-free vector field, admitting a Lipschitz Lagrangian flow
\big\{y_{t}\big\}_{t\in(-\infty,\infty)}
, as in (
51
). Moreover,
y_{0}=\mathrm{Id}
, while
y_{2\max\{W,H\}}
is exactly a half rotation of
Q
around its centre.

This admits the Lagrangian flow
\big\{y_{t}^{-1}\big\}_{t\in(-\infty,\infty)}
, and so enjoys the same properties as above.

Next, we piece these half-rotations together to perform orientation-preserving swaps. This is exactly the building block required to perform the binary swaps (
43
). Let
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be again an open rectangle with width
W>0
and height
H>0
. Let
L\in\mathbb{N}
and suppose in addition that
H
is an integer multiple of
2^{1-L}
, and
W\geq 2^{1-L}
.

Subsequently, we define a time-dependent vector field, symbolically notated by
\vbox{\hbox{\hbox to29.25pt{\vbox to7.91pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

, by

This has the property that at time
t=3W
the left and right halves are swapped in an orientation preserving manner. This is illustrated below:

Finally, suppose
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\in\{1,...,2^{\left\lfloor k/2\right\rfloor}\}
, and
L\in\mathbb{N}
, with
L\geq k+1
.

Let
J_{k,n}=\left[(n-1)2^{-\left\lfloor k/2\right\rfloor},n2^{-\left\lfloor k/2\right\rfloor}\right]\subset\mathbb{T}
.

For
W=2^{-k}
,
H=1
, and
L\geq k+1
we may use

Note that
u^{(1,k,n;L)}
is piecewise given by (
50
), and so is 1-bounded and divergence-free.

By construction (
43
), (
45
) are satisfied, while (
47
) follows from the illustration (
52
) after noting the bound
2^{-L}\leq 2^{1-k}
.

Finally, to prove (
46
), notice in (
52
) we alternate between

and

, and so for all
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
,
t\in[0,3\cdot 2^{-k}]

u^{(1,k,n;L)}(x,t)=-u^{(1,k,n;L)}(x+2^{-L}e_{2},t).

The proof of (
46
) now follows the same argument as the proof of (
22
).
∎

Next, we show the well-posedness of (
TE
) along these vector fields. As for Proposition
5.3
, a weaker version of the following result, when
f\in L^{\infty}L^{\infty}
, follows directly from the well-posedness theory of Ambrosio,
[
4
]
. However, for the sake of completeness, we again prefer to give a direct elementary proof of uniqueness, valid for all
f\in L^{1}L^{1}
.

Proposition 6.3

(Binary-swap uniqueness)
.

Suppose
i\in\{1,2\}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\in\{1,...,2^{\left\lfloor k/2\right\rfloor}\}
,
L\in\mathbb{N}
, with
L\geq k+1
.

Suppose
f\in L^{1}L^{1}
is a weak solution to (
TE
) along
u^{(i,k,n;L)}
on the open time interval
(0,3\cdot 2^{-k})
.

Then there exists some
f_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
such that
f
is (a.e. in
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times(0,3\cdot 2^{-k})
) equal to the Lagrangian solution (Definition
2.6
)

(53)

f(\cdot,t)=f_{0}\circ\big(y^{(i,k,n;L)}_{t}\big)^{-1}.

In particular,
f
is a renormalised weak solution to (
TE
) (Definition
2.4
) along
u^{(i,k,n;L)}
with initial data
f_{0}
. Moreover
f_{0}\circ\big(y^{(i,k,n;L)}_{t}\big)^{-1}\in C^{0}([0,3\cdot 2^{-k}];L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
.

Proof.

We shall give only a brief proof since this essentially follows the proof of Proposition
5.3
. Alternatively, one may be able to prove the result via the
L^{1}([0,T];BV)
-theory of transport developed by Ambrosio in
[
4
]
.

The case
i=2
follows from the same result for
i=1
and the definitions (
48
), (
49
). Hence, we may assume
i=1
.

Notice, from the construction (
52
), that
u^{(1,k,n;L)}
is stationary/time-independent on the time intervals
I_{1}=[0,2\cdot 2^{-k}]
, and
I_{2}=[2\cdot 2^{-k},3\cdot 2^{-k}]
.

We claim it is sufficient to prove (
53
) for some
f_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for a.e.
t\in I_{1}
, and for some
f_{0}^{\prime}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
for a.e.
t\in I_{2}
. This is because, after we prove the continuity
f_{0}\circ\big(y^{(1,k,n;L)}_{t}\big)^{-1}\in C^{0}([0,3\cdot 2^{-k}];L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
, we apply Theorem
3.1
to deduce
f_{0}=f_{0}^{\prime}
.

Restrict now to one of the time intervals
t\in I_{1}
or
t\in I_{2}
. Then we may divide
\mathbb{T}^{2}
into open rectangles
Q\subset\mathbb{T}^{2}
(and a set of Lebesgue-measure zero between rectangles) on which
u^{(1,k,n;L)}
is given either by zero,

, or

.

As in the proof of Proposition
5.3
we take a test function
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(Q\times I_{1})
, supported on
Q\times I_{1}
(or
Q\times I_{2}
respectively), and let
\psi(x,t)=\phi\big(\big(y^{(1,k,n;L)}_{t}\big)^{-1}(x),t\big)
. From the Lipschitz bound (
51
),
\psi\in L^{\infty}(Q\times I_{1})
is then Lipschitz (so may be taken as a test function in (
TE
)), supported on
Q
, and by chain rule the following point-wise equality holds

\left(\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial t}+u^{(1,k,n;L)}\cdot\nabla\psi\right)(x,t)=\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}\left(\big(y^{(1,k,n;L)}_{t}\big)^{-1}(x),t\right).

Following the argument as in Proposition
5.3
then proves (
53
) holds a.e. on each
Q\times I_{1}
, for some
f_{0}
. By gluing together the
f_{0}
for each
Q
, we show that (
53
) holds a.e. on
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times I_{1}
(or
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times I_{2}
respectively).

We are left to show
f_{0}\circ\big(y^{(1,k,n;L)}_{t}\big)^{-1}\in C^{0}([0,3\cdot 2^{-k}];L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
. As in the proof of Proposition
5.3
, this will follow from the global Lipschitz in time bound. That is we must show, for all
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
, and
t,s\in[0,3\cdot 2^{-k}]
,

\left|y^{(1,k,n;L)}_{t}\circ\big(y^{(1,k,n;L)}_{s}\big)^{-1}(x)-x\right|\leq C|t-s|.

This follows by taking
x=x^{\prime}=\big(y^{(1,k,n;L)}_{s}\big)^{-1}(z)
and applying the local Lipschitz bound (
51
).

As discussed, by Theorem
3.1
we may glue together
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times I_{1}
and
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times I_{2}
, proving (
53
).
f
is now a Lagrangian solution to (
TE
) along
u^{(1,k,n;L)}
with initial data
f_{0}
, and so a renormalised weak solution by Remark
2.7
.
∎

Next, we define the times at which to apply a binary swap
y^{(i,k,n;L)}_{3\cdot 2^{-k}}
. We use a quadruple index
(k,m,i,n)\in\mathcal{N}\subset\mathbb{N}^{4}
where
k,i,n
shall determine which binary swap
y^{(i,k,n;L)}_{3\cdot 2^{-k}}
to perform, with
L\in\mathbb{N}
,
L\geq k+1
chosen later, while
m
denotes the
m
th
occurrence of that binary swap. To define the times
t_{(k,m,i,n)}\in[0,1]
at which the binary swaps will be applied, we first define an ordering
<_{\mathrm{time}}
on the indexing set
\mathcal{N}
, and then define
t_{(k,m,i,n)}
so that they respect this ordering. The resulting time ordering is illustrated in the diagram (
57
) below, with the ordering designed to iteratively make
4^{m}
copies of the initial data on a square lattice with widths
2^{-m}
, thus mixing the initial data to its spatial average as
m\to\infty
.

We additionally define a well-order
<_{\mathrm{lex}}
on the indexing set
\mathcal{N}
. This will inform which binary swaps
y^{(i,k,n;L)}_{3\cdot 2^{-k}}
will be included when approximating the vanishing viscosity limit of (

ν

-ADE
), as in Proposition
4.3
.

Definition 6.4

(Total-orders
<_{\mathrm{lex}}
,
<_{\mathrm{time}}
)
.

For any
p\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the lexicographic well-order
<_{\mathrm{lex}}
on
\mathbb{N}^{p}
via

a<_{\mathrm{lex}}b\iff(\exists i\in\{1,...,p\}),\;\begin{cases}(\forall j<i),\;a_{j}=b_{j},\\
a_{i}<b_{i}.\end{cases}

Let

\mathcal{N}=\left\{(k,m,i,n)\in\mathbb{N}^{4}:\;m\leq k,i\in\{1,2\},\;n\leq 2^{\left\lfloor k/2\right\rfloor}\right\},

then note that the well-order
(\mathcal{N},<_{\mathrm{lex}})
is order isomorphic to
\mathbb{N}
with the usual order.

We also define another total-order
<_{\mathrm{time}}
on
\mathcal{N}
, via

(54)

(k_{1},m_{1},i_{1},n_{1})<_{\mathrm{time}}(k_{2},m_{2},i_{2},n_{2})\text{ if }(m_{1},k_{2},i_{2},n_{2})<_{\mathrm{lex}}(m_{2},k_{1},i_{1},n_{1}),

which is not a well-order.

Define also for each
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{N}
the finite subset

\mathcal{N_{K}}=\{(k,m,i,n)\in\mathcal{N}:(k,m,i,n)\leq_{\mathrm{lex}}\mathcal{K}\},

which inherits the finite (and therefore well-) orders
(\mathcal{N_{K}},<_{\mathrm{lex}})
,
(\mathcal{N_{K}},<_{\mathrm{time}})
.

For each
(k,m,i,n)\in\mathcal{N}
we define

(55)

T_{(k,m,i,n)}=\sum_{(k^{\prime},m^{\prime},i^{\prime},n^{\prime})<_{\mathrm{time}}(k,m,i,n)}3\cdot 2^{-k^{\prime}}<42,

where the bound on
T_{(k,m,i,n)}
follows from direct calculation of

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}2k\cdot 2^{\left\lfloor k/2\right\rfloor}\cdot 3\cdot 2^{-k}=42,

and an empty sum is zero.

Moreover, for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, let

(56)

T_{m}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m^{\prime}\leq m\\
(k^{\prime},m^{\prime},i^{\prime},n^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}\end{subarray}}3\cdot 2^{-k^{\prime}}<42.

Writing also
T_{0}=0
, we illustrate the time ordering
<_{\mathrm{time}}
in the following diagram,

(57)

T_{0}
T_{1}
T_{2}
T_{3}
T_{(1,1,i,n)}
T_{(2,1,i,n)}
T_{(3,1,i,n)}
T_{(4,1,i,n)}
...
T_{(2,2,i,n)}
T_{(3,2,i,n)}
T_{(4,2,i,n)}
...
T_{(3,3,i,n)}
T_{(4,3,i,n)}
...
...

where for fixed
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\leq k
, the bracket
T_{(k,m,i,n)}
contains for all
i\in\{1,2\}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\leq 2^{\left\lfloor k/2\right\rfloor}
, precisely the time intervals

\left[T_{(k,m,i,n)},T_{(k,m,i,n)}+3\cdot 2^{-k}\right).

Next, we apply the divergence-free vector fields
u^{\left(i,k,n;L\right)}
(for some
L\in\mathbb{N}
to be chosen) in each time interval
\left[T_{(k,m,i,n)},T_{(k,m,i,n)}+3\cdot 2^{-k}\right)
, with
m\in\{1,...,k\}
an index denoting the
m
th
occurrence of this swap. The order is chosen so that the solution to (
TE
) at time
t=T_{m}
creates
4^{m}
identical copies of the initial data on a square lattice with widths
2^{-m}
.

To see how this can be achieved,
i\in\{1,2\}
denotes which coordinate the binary swap (
43
) acts on, while
n\in\{1,...,2^{\left\lfloor k/2\right\rfloor}\}
is an integer denoting which region of
\mathbb{T}^{2}
the binary swap is completed on.

For fixed
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\{1,...,k\}
, these binary swap commute, and together the bracket
T_{(k,m,i,n)}
swaps the
k
th
and
(k+1)
th
binary digit of both coordinates of every
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
.

The order
\leq_{\mathrm{lex}}
is chosen such that between
T_{m-1}
and
T_{m}
, an ‘undefined’ binary digit passes all the way from infinitely far up the binary expansion, down to the
m
th
position. Since its values are undefined, this creates four copies (2 in each coordinate) of the
t=T_{m-1}
data at
t=T_{m}
. See Proposition
6.6
below for the details.

But first, we define this vector field and its finite approximations, which by Theorem
4.3
will control the vanishing viscosity limit of (

ν

-ADE
).

Definition 6.5

(Gradual perfect mixing)
.

We follow the notation introduced in Definition
6.4
.

Notice that a particular
\mathcal{P}=(k,m,i,n)\in\mathcal{N}
fixes
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
,
n\in\{1,...,2^{\left\lfloor k/2\right\rfloor}\}
.

For
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{N}
, and a finite sequence
\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N_{K}}}\subset\mathbb{N}
with
L_{(k,m,i,n)}\geq k+1
for all
(k,m,i,n)\in\mathcal{N_{K}}
, we define the following time-dependent vector field.

\displaystyle u_{\mathcal{K}}^{\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N_{K}}}}:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,50]\to\mathbb{R}^{2},

\displaystyle u_{\mathcal{K}}^{\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N_{K}}}}(x,t)=\begin{cases}u^{\left(i,k,n;L_{\mathcal{P}}\right)}(x,t-T_{\mathcal{P}})&\text{for }\begin{cases}\mathcal{P}=(k,m,i,n)\in\mathcal{N_{K}},\text{ and}\\
t\in\left[T_{\mathcal{P}},T_{\mathcal{P}}+3\cdot 2^{-k}\right),\end{cases}\\
0&\text{otherwise, in particular for $t\geq 42$}.\end{cases}

Meanwhile, for an infinite sequence
\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N}}\subset\mathbb{N}
with
L_{(k,m,i,n)}\geq k+1
for all
(k,m,i,n)\in\mathcal{N}
, we define the following time-dependent vector field.

\displaystyle u_{\infty}^{\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N}}}:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,50]\to\mathbb{R}^{2},

\displaystyle u_{\infty}^{\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N}}}(x,t)=\begin{cases}u^{\left(i,k,n;L_{\mathcal{P}}\right)}(x,t-T_{\mathcal{P}})&\text{for }\begin{cases}\mathcal{P}=(k,m,i,n)\in\mathcal{N},\text{ and}\\
t\in\left[T_{\mathcal{P}},T_{\mathcal{P}}+3\cdot 2^{-k}\right),\end{cases}\\
0&\text{otherwise, in particular for $t\geq 42$}.\end{cases}

These vector fields are well-defined since by (
55
), the time intervals

\left[T_{(k,m,i,n)},T_{(k,m,i,n)}+3\cdot 2^{-k}\right),

are disjoint subsets of
[0,42]
.

Proposition 6.6

(Mixing)
.

We follow the notation introduced in Definitions
6.4
,
6.5
.

Let
f_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
, and suppose we have an infinite sequence
\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N}}\subset\mathbb{N}
with
L_{(k,m,i,n)}\geq k+1
for all
(k,m,i,n)\in\mathcal{N}
. Then for each
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{N}
there exists a unique weak solution
f^{\mathcal{K}}
to (
TE
) along
u_{\mathcal{K}}^{\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N_{K}}}}
with initial data
f_{0}
. Moreover,
f^{\mathcal{K}}\in(C^{0}L^{1})\cap(L^{\infty}L^{\infty})
, is a Lagrangian solution, and hence also a renormalised weak solution (Definitions
2.4
,
2.6
).

By the well-order
(\mathcal{N},<_{\mathrm{lex}})
(which is order isomorphic to
(\mathbb{N},<)
), as
\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{lex}}\infty
,

f^{\mathcal{K}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{K}\to\infty}f^{\infty},

with the above convergence in weak-
*

L^{\infty}L^{\infty}
, strong in
L^{p}([0,42];L^{p})
and
\left.C^{0}([0,42-\epsilon];L^{p})\right.
for all
p\in[1,\infty)
, and all
\epsilon>0
. The limit function
f^{\infty}\in C_{\mathrm{weak-}*}^{0}L^{\infty}
is a weak solution to (
TE
) along
u_{\infty}^{\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N}}}
with initial data
f_{0}
.

Moreover, for all
t\in[42,50]

(58)

f^{\infty}(\cdot,t)\equiv\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}f_{0}(y)\;dy,

i.e.,
f^{\infty}(\cdot,t)
is perfectly mixed after
t=42
.

Proof.

Recall the language and notation introduced in Definitions
6.2
,
6.4
,
6.5
, as well as Definitions
2.4
,
2.6
of renormalised and Lagrangian solutions to (
TE
).

By Theorems
3.1
,
3.4
, there exists some weak solution
f^{\mathcal{K}}\in C_{\mathrm{weak-}*}^{0}L^{\infty}
to (
TE
) along
u_{\mathcal{K}}^{\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N_{K}}}}
with initial data
f_{0}
.

We aim to show
f\in C^{0}L^{1}
with

(59)

f^{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot,t)=f_{0}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}}\big)^{-1},

for
\big\{\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}}\big\}_{t\in[0,50]}
a Lagrangian flow along
u_{\mathcal{K}}^{\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N_{K}}}}
which does not depend on
f_{0}
, thus proving uniqueness.

From the definition of
u_{\mathcal{K}}^{\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N_{K}}}}
, finiteness of the set
\mathcal{N_{K}}
, and disjointness of the intervals
\left[T_{(k,m,i,n)},T_{(k,m,i,n)}+3\cdot 2^{-k}\right)
for all
(k,m,i,n)\in\mathcal{N_{K}}
, we may piecewise apply Proposition
6.3
to
f^{\mathcal{K}}
. We use that
f^{\mathcal{K}}\in C_{\mathrm{weak-}*}^{0}L^{\infty}
to glue together the pieces, and that any weak solution to (
TE
) along
u\equiv 0
on an open time interval
I
is a constant function of
t\in I
, see for example Theorem 3.1.4’ in
[
31
]
. This constructs for each
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{N}
a Lagrangian flow
\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}}
satisfying (
59
), and moreover gives an expression for
\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}}
in terms of the binary swaps
y_{t}^{(i,k,n;L)}
, see (
61
) below.

For each
\mathcal{P}=(k,m,i,n)\in\mathcal{N_{K}}
, define

T_{\mathrm{suc}(\mathcal{P})}=\begin{cases}50&\text{if $\mathcal{P}$ is maximal in $(\mathcal{N_{K}},<_{\mathrm{time}})$},\;\text{else}\\
T_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}&\text{for $\mathcal{P}^{\prime}$ the successor of $\mathcal{P}$ in $(\mathcal{N_{K}},<_{\mathrm{time}})$}.\end{cases}

With each
\mathcal{P}=(k,m,i,n)\in\mathcal{N_{K}}
fixed, and therefore fixed
k\in\mathbb{N}
, by (
55
) we have that

(60)

T_{\mathcal{P}}+3\cdot 2^{-k}\leq T_{\mathrm{suc}(\mathcal{P})}.

With each
\mathcal{P}=(k,m,i,n)\in\mathcal{N_{K}}
fixed, and therefore fixed
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
,
n\in\{1,...,2^{\left\lfloor k/2\right\rfloor}\}
, we have that for all
t\in[T_{\mathcal{P}},T_{\mathrm{suc}(\mathcal{P})}]

(61)

\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{T_{\mathcal{P}}}^{\mathcal{K}}\big)^{-1}=\begin{cases}y_{t-T_{\mathcal{P}}}^{\left(i,k,n;L_{\mathcal{P}}\right)}&\text{if }t\in[T_{\mathcal{P}},T_{\mathcal{P}}+3\cdot 2^{-k}],\\
y_{3\cdot 2^{-k}}^{\left(i,k,n;L_{\mathcal{P}}\right)}&\text{if }t\in[T_{\mathcal{P}}+3\cdot 2^{-k},T_{\mathrm{suc}(\mathcal{P})}].\end{cases}

Meanwhile, if
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}
is minimal in
(\mathcal{N_{K}},<_{\mathrm{time}})
, we have that for all
t\in[0,T_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{min}}}]
,
\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}}=\mathrm{Id}
. This completes the proof of uniqueness.

Next, we wish to show, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, that the sequence
f^{\mathcal{K}}\in C^{0}L^{1}
is Cauchy in
C^{0}([T_{m-1},T_{m}];L^{1})
as
\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{lex}}\infty
, as follows.

We proceed by induction on
m\in\mathbb{N}
. By the inductive hypothesis we have that
f^{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot,T_{m-1})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
is Cauchy in
L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
as
\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{lex}}\infty
(for the base case
m=1
this follows by
f^{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot,T_{0})\equiv f_{0}
). From this we wish to deduce that
f^{\mathcal{K}}\in C^{0}L^{1}
is Cauchy in
C^{0}([T_{m-1},T_{m}];L^{1})
as
\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{lex}}\infty
. Before we proceed with the induction we will need some properties of the Lagrangian flow
\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}}
on the interval
t\in[T_{m-1},T_{m}]
.

Fix any
(k,m,i,n)\in\mathcal{N}
, which fixes
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\{1,...,k\}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
,
n\in\{1,...,2^{\left\lfloor k/2\right\rfloor}\}
.

Recall the definition (
56
) of
T_{m}
and
T_{m-1}
, where we set
T_{0}=0
if necessary. By (
54
), we have (it may be helpful to consider the illustration (
57
))

\displaystyle T_{m-1}<T_{(k,m,i,n)},

\displaystyle T_{(k,m,i,n)}+3\cdot 2^{-k}\leq T_{m},

and also for each
\mathcal{P}=(k^{\prime},m^{\prime},i^{\prime},n^{\prime})>_{\mathrm{lex}}(k,m,i,n)
, which fixes
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
,
m^{\prime}\in\{1,...,k^{\prime}\}
,

\displaystyle T_{\mathcal{P}}+3\cdot 2^{-k^{\prime}}\leq T_{(k,m,i,n)}\quad\text{if }m^{\prime}\leq m,

\displaystyle T_{m}<T_{\mathcal{P}}\quad\text{if }m<m^{\prime}.

Therefore, by the piecewise structure (
61
), for each
t\in[T_{(k,m,i,n)},T_{m}]
, and for each
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}>_{\mathrm{lex}}\mathcal{K}\geq_{\mathrm{lex}}(k,m,i,n)
, we have that

\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{T_{(k,m,i,n)}}^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}\big)^{-1}=\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{T_{(k,m,i,n)}}^{\mathcal{K}}\big)^{-1},

is unchanged. We shall need the inverse of these Lagrangian maps,

(62)

\tilde{y}_{T_{(k,m,i,n)}}^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}\big)^{-1}=\tilde{y}_{T_{(k,m,i,n)}}^{\mathcal{K}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}}\big)^{-1}.

Next, for each
\mathcal{P}=(k^{\prime},m^{\prime},i^{\prime},n^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}
with
k^{\prime}<k
, which fixes
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
,
m^{\prime}\in\{1,...,k^{\prime}\}
, we have that

\displaystyle T_{\mathcal{P}}+3\cdot 2^{-k^{\prime}}\leq T_{m-1}\quad\text{if }m^{\prime}<m,

\displaystyle T_{(k,m,i,n)}<T_{\mathcal{P}}\quad\text{if }m\leq m^{\prime}.

Therefore, we may apply (
47
) and the piecewise structure (
61
) to show the following.

For any
r,r^{\prime}\in\{1,...,2^{k-1}\}
, denote the intervals
J=[(r-1)2^{1-k},r2^{1-k}]
,
J^{\prime}=[(r^{\prime}-1)2^{1-k},r^{\prime}2^{1-k}]
.

For all
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{N}
, for all
t\in[T_{m-1},T_{(k,m,i,n)}]
, we see that the Lagrangian-flow
\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{T_{m-1}}^{\mathcal{K}}\big)^{-1}
preserves the square lattice

(63)

\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{T_{m-1}}^{\mathcal{K}}\big)^{-1}:J\times J^{\prime}\leftrightarrow J\times J^{\prime}.

We are now ready to proceed with the induction. Recall we have assumed that for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
f^{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot,T_{m-1})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
is Cauchy in
L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
as
\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{lex}}\infty
.

We wish to deduce
f^{\mathcal{K}}\in C^{0}L^{1}
is Cauchy in
C^{0}([T_{m-1},T_{m}];L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
as
\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{lex}}\infty
.

Denote by
f_{m-1}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
the limit
f^{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot,T_{m-1})\xrightarrow{\mathcal{K}\to\infty}f_{m-1}
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
.

Note, by (
59
), for all
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{N}
, and for all
t\in[T_{m-1},T_{m}]
, we may write

(64)

f^{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot,t)=f^{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot,T_{m-1})\circ\tilde{y}_{T_{m-1}}^{\mathcal{K}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}}\big)^{-1}.

Fix some
\epsilon>0
. Let
\phi_{\epsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
be a smooth approximation of
f_{m-1}
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
so that
\left\|\phi_{\epsilon}-f_{m-1}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\leq\epsilon
.

Fixing also some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{N}
be large enough that we have
\mathcal{K}\geq_{\mathrm{lex}}(k,m,1,1)
, and that for all
\mathcal{K^{\prime}}\geq_{\mathrm{lex}}\mathcal{K}
,

\left\|f^{\mathcal{K^{\prime}}}(\cdot,T_{m-1})-f_{m-1}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\leq\epsilon.

Then, it also follows that

\left\|f^{\mathcal{K^{\prime}}}(\cdot,T_{m-1})-\phi_{\epsilon}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\leq 2\epsilon.

Therefore, by (
64
), and since Lagrangian flows are Lebesgue-measure preserving, for any
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\geq_{\mathrm{lex}}\mathcal{K}
, and for all
t\in[T_{m-1},T_{m}]
, we have that

\left\|f^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}(\cdot,t)-f^{\mathcal{K}}(\cdot,t)\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\leq 4\epsilon+\left\|\phi_{\epsilon}\circ\tilde{y}_{T_{m-1}}^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}\big)^{-1}-\phi_{\epsilon}\circ\tilde{y}_{T_{m-1}}^{\mathcal{K}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}}\big)^{-1}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}.

We now split into two cases,
t\in[T_{m-1},T_{(k,m,1,1)}]
, and
t\in[T_{(k,m,1,1)},T_{m}]
. We first consider the former.

For the square lattice of widths
2^{1-k}
,
J\times J^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{T}^{2}
defined in (
63
), we have for all
x,y\in J\times J^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\left|\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi_{\epsilon}(y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}|x-y|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\left\|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}2^{1-k}.

So by (
63
), for all
t\in[T_{m-1},T_{(k,m,1,1)}]
, and for all
\mathcal{K^{\prime}}\geq_{\mathrm{lex}}\mathcal{K}
, we have that

(65)

\left\|\phi_{\epsilon}\circ\tilde{y}_{T_{m-1}}^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}\big)^{-1}-\phi_{\epsilon}\circ\tilde{y}_{T_{m-1}}^{\mathcal{K}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}}\big)^{-1}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\leq\sqrt{2}\left\|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}2^{1-k}.

Next, by (
62
), for all
t\in[T_{(k,m,1,1)},T_{m}]
, we have that

\left\|\phi_{\epsilon}\circ\tilde{y}_{T_{m-1}}^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}\big)^{-1}-\phi_{\epsilon}\circ\tilde{y}_{T_{m-1}}^{\mathcal{K}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}}\big)^{-1}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\\
\begin{aligned} &=\left\|\phi_{\epsilon}\circ\tilde{y}_{T_{m-1}}^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{T_{(k,m,1,1)}}^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}\big)^{-1}\circ\tilde{y}_{T_{(k,m,1,1)}}^{\mathcal{K}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}}\big)^{-1}-\phi_{\epsilon}\circ\tilde{y}_{T_{m-1}}^{\mathcal{K}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{t}^{\mathcal{K}}\big)^{-1}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}\\
&=\left\|\phi_{\epsilon}\circ\tilde{y}_{T_{m-1}}^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{T_{(k,m,1,1)}}^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}\big)^{-1}-\phi_{\epsilon}\circ\tilde{y}_{T_{m-1}}^{\mathcal{K}}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{T_{(k,m,1,1)}}^{\mathcal{K}}\big)^{-1}\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2})},\end{aligned}

where the last line is already bounded in (
65
), by
\sqrt{2}\left\|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}2^{1-k}
. Putting everything together, we see that

\left\|f^{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}-f^{\mathcal{K}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}([T_{m-1},T_{m}];L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))}\leq 4\epsilon+\sqrt{2}\left\|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}2^{1-k}.

\left\|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})}
depends only on
\epsilon
and
f_{m-1}
, and in particular not on
k
. Thus for
k
sufficiently large, i.e. for
\mathcal{K^{\prime}},\mathcal{K}\in(\mathcal{N},<_{\mathrm{lex}})
sufficiently large, we can make the right-hand side arbitrarily small. Therefore
f^{\mathcal{K}}
is Cauchy in
L^{\infty}([T_{m-1},T_{m}];L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}))
as
\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{lex}}\infty
, as required.

This completes the induction. Observe that
T_{m}\xrightarrow{m\to\infty}42
, and so we have proven that
f^{\mathcal{K}}
converges in
C^{0}([0,42-\epsilon];L^{1})
as
\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{lex}}\infty
, for any
\epsilon>0
.

f^{\mathcal{K}}
are Lagrangian (and weak) solutions to (
TE
) along
u_{\mathcal{K}}^{\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N}}}
with initial data
f_{0}
. Therefore, since
f_{0}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2})
,
f^{\mathcal{K}}
are uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}L^{\infty}
.

Moreover, by Definition
6.5
,
u_{\mathcal{K}}^{\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N_{K}}}}(\cdot,t)
is eventually constant, and equal to
u_{\infty}^{\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N}}}(\cdot,t)
as
\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{lex}}\infty
for each
t\in[0,50]
, and are uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}L^{\infty}
.

Denote by
\bar{f}
a weak-
*
limit point of
\{f^{\mathcal{K}}\}_{\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{N}}
in
L^{\infty}([0,50];L^{\infty})
, that is there exists a subsequence
\mathcal{K}_{n}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\infty
with
f^{\mathcal{K}_{n}}\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\bar{f}
in weak-
*

L^{\infty}L^{\infty}
. Then for all
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,50))
, we see that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,50]}\bar{f}\left(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}+u_{\infty}^{\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N}}}\cdot\nabla\phi\right)\;dxdt

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,50]}f^{\mathcal{K}_{n}}\left(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}+u_{\infty}^{\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N}}}\cdot\nabla\phi\right)\;dxdt

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,50]}f^{\mathcal{K}_{n}}\left(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}+u_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}^{\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{K}_{n}}}}\cdot\nabla\phi\right)\;dxdt

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}f_{0}\phi_{0}\;dx.

Therefore,
\bar{f}
is a weak solution to (
TE
) along
u_{\infty}^{\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N}}}
with initial data
f_{0}
. Moreover, it is bounded in
L^{\infty}L^{\infty}
and so by Theorem
3.1
the limit is in
C_{\mathrm{weak-}*}^{0}([0,50];L^{\infty})
. However,
u_{\infty}^{\{L_{\mathcal{P}}\}_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathcal{N}}}(\cdot,t)\equiv 0
for each
t\in[42,50]
and so (say by (
1
)), for all
t\in[42,50]
,
\bar{f}(\cdot,t)
is determined by
\bar{f}(\cdot,s)
for
s\in[0,42)
.

However, we have already shown
\underset{\mathcal{K}\to\infty}{\lim}f^{\mathcal{K}}
converges in
C^{0}([0,42-\epsilon];L^{1})
for any
\epsilon>0
. Therefore, the limit
\bar{f}
is unique. Assume then that
\underset{\mathcal{K}\to\infty}{\lim}f^{\mathcal{K}}
does not converge in weak-
*

L^{\infty}([0,50];L^{\infty})
. Then, by the uniform bound in
L^{\infty}([0,50];L^{\infty})
, there exist at least two limit points, contradicting uniqueness.

Denote now the limit by
f^{\infty}
, that is
f^{\mathcal{K}}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{K}\to\infty}f^{\infty}
in weak-
*

L^{\infty}([0,50];L^{\infty})
and strongly in
C^{0}([0,42-\epsilon];L^{p})
for all
\epsilon>0
.

We are left to show convergence in
L^{p}([0,42];L^{p})
and
C^{0}([0,42-\epsilon];L^{p})
for all
p\in[1,\infty)
, and all
\epsilon>0
. The latter of these follows by interpolation between convergence in
C^{0}([0,42-\epsilon];L^{1})
and the existing uniform bound in
L^{\infty}L^{\infty}
. When combined with the uniform bound in
L^{2}([0,42];L^{2})
this further implies convergence of the norm
\left\|f^{\mathcal{K}}\right\|_{L^{2}([0,42];L^{2})}
. Convergence in
L^{2}([0,42];L^{2})
then follows from the already proved weak convergence in
L^{2}([0,42];L^{2})
. The analogous result for
p\in[1,2)
follows from compactness of the domain
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times[0,42]
. Moreover, the convergence for
p\in(2,\infty)
follows by interpolation with the existing uniform bound in
L^{\infty}([0,42];L^{\infty})
.

Finally, we show the mixing formula (
58
).

For
K\in\mathbb{N}
denote by
f^{K}=f^{(K,K,2,2^{\left\lfloor K/2\right\rfloor})}
,
\mathcal{N}_{K}=\mathcal{N}_{(K,K,2,2^{\left\lfloor K/2\right\rfloor})}
, and by
\tilde{y}_{t}^{K}=\tilde{y}_{t}^{(K,K,2,2^{\left\lfloor K/2\right\rfloor})}
.

By (
54
), observe that
(k,m,i,n)\in\mathcal{N}_{K}
if and only if
k\leq K
, and moreover
(K,K,2,2^{\left\lfloor K/2\right\rfloor})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{lex}}\infty
as
K\to\infty
.

Fix
k,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\leq K
,
m\in\{1,...,k\}
.
For all
i\in\{1,2\}
, for all
n\in\{1,...,2^{\left\lfloor k/2\right\rfloor}\}
, we see that
(k,m,i,n)\in\mathcal{N}_{K}
, and so consider the maps

\tilde{y}_{T_{(k,m,i,n)}+3\cdot 2^{-k}}^{K}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{T_{(k,m,i,n)}}^{K}\big)^{-1}.

By (
43
), (
60
), (
61
), these maps commute for all
i\in\{1,2\}
, for all
n\in\{1,...,2^{\left\lfloor k/2\right\rfloor}\}
.

Moreover, by (
54
), (
55
) (illustrated in (
57
)), their composition is equal to

\tilde{y}_{T_{(k,m,1,1)}+3\cdot 2^{-k}}^{K}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{T_{(k,m,2,2^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor})}}^{K}\big)^{-1}.

Furthermore, we have the following expression for this map in terms of binary expansions.

For
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
denote by
(x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2})=\tilde{y}_{T_{(k,m,1,1)}+3\cdot 2^{-k}}^{K}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{T_{(k,m,2,2^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor})}}^{K}\big)^{-1}(x)
.

For a.e.
x\in\mathbb{T}^{2}
, for all
j\in\{1,2\}
,
x^{\prime}_{j}
has swapping the
k
th
and
(k+1)
th
binary digits of
x_{j}
, illustrated by the map
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}Y_{k}}
below. For the convenience of the reader, we use colour to denote swaps in the binary expansion. Following the notation for binary expansions introduced in (
42
), we have that for
j\in\{1,2\}
the coordinate, and for
l\in\mathbb{N}
the binary digit,

\displaystyle\left(\tilde{y}_{T_{(k,m,1,1)}+3\cdot 2^{-k}}^{K}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{T_{(k,m,2,2^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor})}}^{K}\big)^{-1}(x)\right)_{j}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{48.95717pt}{21.16318pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{

\displaystyle\left(\tilde{y}_{T_{(k,m,1,1)}+3\cdot 2^{-k}}^{K}\circ\big(\tilde{y}_{T_{(k,m,2,2^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor})}}^{K}\big)^{-1}(x)\right)_{j,l}
